Trace [25–26]
Matrix trace and similarity
Jordan normal form has shown how much information about an operator can be made
visible by choosing a suitable basis. One particularly simple piece of information is the
sum of the diagonal entries of a representing matrix.
Definition 41 (LADR 8.47). Let A = (aij )i,j be an n-by-n matrix. The trace
of A is
tr(A) = a11 + · · · + ann.
For example,
tr


1 2 3
4 5 6
7 8 9

 = 1 + 5 + 9 = 15.
The trace has a useful compatibility with matrix multiplication.
Proposition 78 (LADR 8.49). Let A be an m-by-n matrix and B an n-by-m
matrix. Then
tr(AB) = tr(BA).
Proof. Writing A = (aij ) and B = (bji), we have
tr(AB) = Xm
i=1
(AB)ii
=
Xm
i=1
Xn
j=1
aij bji
=
Xn
j=1
Xm
i=1
bjiaij
=
Xn
j=1
(BA)jj
= tr(BA).
125

In particular, trace is unchanged by similarity. If P is invertible, then
tr(P
−1AP) = tr(AP P −1
) = tr(A).
Thus different matrices representing the same operator have the same trace.
Trace of an operator
Definition 42 (LADR 8.50, 8.51). Let V be a finite-dimensional vector space
and let T ∈ L(V ). Choose any basis B of V . The trace of T is
tr(T) = trMB(T)

.
The similarity calculation above shows that this number does not depend on the
choice of basis.
The definition is therefore coordinate-independent even though it is calculated from
coordinates. Once a convenient basis is available, the trace can often be read off immediately.
Proposition 79 (LADR 8.52). Let T ∈ L(V ), where V is a finite-dimensional
complex vector space. Then
tr(T)
is the sum of the eigenvalues of T, counted with algebraic multiplicity.
Proof. Choose a basis in which T is in Jordan normal form. The diagonal entries of
this matrix are precisely the eigenvalues of T, each repeated according to its algebraic
multiplicity. The trace is the sum of these diagonal entries.
Equivalently, if the distinct eigenvalues are
λ1, . . . , λr
with algebraic multiplicities m1, . . . , mr, then
tr(T) = m1λ1 + · · · + mrλr.
Thus the trace gives one simple summary of the spectral data of an operator.
Example 64. Consider
A =


1 1 1
1 2 4
1 3 9

 .
Its eigenvalues are not especially pleasant to write down individually, but their sum is
immediate:
λ1 + λ2 + λ3 = tr(A) = 1 + 2 + 9 = 12.
126

Characteristic coefficients and commutators
There is also a direct connection with the characteristic polynomial. If dim(V ) = n
and
χT (z) = Yn
j=1
(z − λj ),
where the eigenvalues are listed with algebraic multiplicity, then the coefficient of z
n−1
is
−
Xn
j=1
λj = −tr(T).
The identity tr(ST) = tr(T S) also gives information that does not depend on eigenvalues.
Proposition 80. Let S, T ∈ L(V ). Then
tr(ST − T S) = 0.
In particular, if V is a nonzero finite-dimensional vector space over R or C, there
are no operators S, T such that
ST − T S = I.
Proof. We have
tr(ST − T S) = tr(ST) − tr(T S) = 0.
On the other hand,
tr(I) = dim(V ) > 0
over R or C, so ST − T S cannot equal I.
More generally, the trace of a product is unchanged by a cyclic permutation:
tr(ABC) = tr(BCA) = tr(CAB).
127

Inner product [26–28]
The first part of the course has been algebraic: vector spaces, linear maps, invariant
subspaces and canonical forms require no notion of length or angle. An inner product
adds that geometric structure. It gives us lengths, angles, orthogonality and a preferred
way to compare directions.
The algebraic questions do not disappear. Geometry will let us choose especially
useful bases, and the spectral theorem and singular value decomposition will show
how strongly an operator can simplify when its algebraic and geometric structures
are considered together. An optional section at the end of the chapter develops a
contemporary application: attention in transformer language models. There an inner
product is used as a matching score in a soft retrieval mechanism, and the resulting
stochastic weights determine how information is combined.
Inner product and norm
Throughout this chapter, F is either R or C. When F = R, all definitions are the same,
but the complex conjugations should be ignored.
Definition 43 (LADR 6.2). Let V be a vector space over F. An inner product
on V is a function ⟨−, −⟩ : V × V → F with the following properties:
(i) ⟨−, −⟩ is positive definite: ⟨v, v⟩ > 0 for all v ̸= 0.
(ii) ⟨−, −⟩ is sesquilinear (linear in the first entry and conjugate-linear in the
second):
⟨λu + v, w⟩ = λ⟨u, w⟩ + ⟨v, w⟩, ⟨u, λv + w⟩ = λ⟨u, v⟩ + ⟨u, w⟩;
(iii) ⟨−, −⟩ is conjugate symmetric:
⟨u, v⟩ = ⟨v, u⟩.
Example 65. On C
n
, the dot product is defined by
⟨(w1, ..., wn),(z1, ..., zn)⟩ := w1z1 + ... + wnzn.
It is straightforward to verify conditions (i)-(iii) above.
128

We needed to include complex conjugation to make the inner product positive definite. Trying to carry over the definition of inner product on R
n
to C
n directly would
result in equations like

i 1


i
1

= i
2 + 12 = 0.
Example 66. Let V be the space of continuous complex-valued functions on the interval [0, 1]. Then
⟨f, g⟩ := Z 1
0
f(x)g(x) dx
defines an inner product, called the L
2
-product.
More generally, we can define the L
2
-product on any bounded interval [a, b]. On unbounded intervals such as R, we should modify V to make sure that the indefinite
integral R ∞
−∞ f(x)g(x) dx exists for any f, g ∈ V.
Definition 44. Let V be a vector space over F. A norm on V is a function
∥ · ∥ : V → R with the following properties:
(i) ∥ · ∥ is positive: ∥v∥ > 0 for all v ̸= 0;
(ii) ∥ · ∥ is homogeneous: ∥λv∥ = |λ| · ∥v∥ for any v ∈ V and λ ∈ F, where |λ|
denotes the absolute value;
(iii) ∥ · ∥ satisfies the triangle inequality
∥u + v∥ ≤ ∥u∥ + ∥v∥, u, v ∈ V.
∥ · ∥ is an abstraction of the concept of “length” of a vector. Although for general
vector spaces V , this has no geometric meaning (what is the “length” of a polynomial?’),
we can apply geometric intuition to prove algebraic results in V .
Proposition 81 (LADR 6.7, 6.17). Let V be a vector space with inner product
⟨−, −⟩. Then
∥v∥ := p
⟨v, v⟩
is a norm on V .
Proving the triangle inequality takes a little work. We will use another important
inequality to prove it - the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality:
Proposition 82 (LADR 6.14). Let V be a vector space with inner product ⟨−, −⟩
and induced norm ∥ · ∥. Then
|⟨u, v⟩| ≤ ∥u∥ · ∥v∥, u, v ∈ V,
and equality holds if and only if u, v is linearly dependent.

We can now finish the proof that the induced norm satisfies the norm axioms.
Proof. (i) Since ⟨−, −⟩ is positive definite, ∥ · ∥ is also positive definite.
(ii) For any λ ∈ F and v ∈ V,
∥λv∥
2 = ⟨λv, λv⟩ = λλ⟨v, v⟩ = |λ|2
∥v∥
2
,
130

so ∥λv∥ = |λ| · ∥v∥.
(iii) For any u, v ∈ V ,
∥u + v∥
2 = ⟨u + v, u + v⟩
= ⟨u, u⟩ + ⟨v, u⟩ + ⟨u, v⟩ + ⟨v, v⟩
= ∥u∥
2 + 2Reh
⟨u, v⟩
i
+ ∥v∥
2
≤ ∥u∥
2 + 2|⟨u, v⟩| + ∥v∥2
≤ ∥u∥
2 + 2∥u∥∥v∥ + ∥v∥2
= (∥u∥ + ∥v∥)
2
,
where the last inequality was the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Therefore,
∥u + v∥ ≤ ∥u∥ + ∥v∥.
Conversely, we can ask when a norm comes from an inner product. It turns out
that there are many norms that do not; for example, on C
n
, the maximum norm
∥(z1, ..., zn)∥∞ := max{|z1|, ..., |zn|}
is a norm but it does not come from a scalar product; for example, it fails the parallelogram law. There is a simple criterion - but proving it would take a little too long.
This is known as the Jordan-von Neumann theorem.
Proposition 83 (LADR 6.21, 6A.28). Let ∥ · ∥ be a norm on a vector space V .
The following are equivalent:
(i) ∥ · ∥ is induced by a scalar product ⟨−, −⟩ on V ;
(ii) The parallelogram law holds:
∥u + v∥
2 + ∥u − v∥2 = 2∥u∥2 + 2∥v∥2
, u, v ∈ V.
In this case, the scalar product is given by the polarization identity:
⟨u, v⟩ =
∥u + v∥
2 − ∥u − v∥2
4
if V is a real vector space, and
⟨u, v⟩ =
∥u + v∥
2 − ∥u − v∥2 + i∥u + iv∥2 − i∥u − iv∥2
4
if V is a complex vector space.
131

Proof. We will only prove the easy direction (i) ⇒ (ii). In this case, the squared norms
are inner products, and we can verify that
⟨u + v, u + v⟩ + ⟨u − v, u − v⟩
=

⟨u, u⟩ + ⟨v, u⟩ + ⟨u, v⟩ + ⟨v, v⟩

+

⟨u, u⟩ − ⟨v, u⟩ − ⟨u, v⟩ + ⟨v, v⟩

= 2 · ⟨u, u⟩ + 2 · ⟨v, v⟩.
In other words, ∥u + v∥
2 + ∥u − v∥2 = 2∥u∥2 + 2∥v∥2
.
Geometrically, the parallelogram law states that the sum of the squares of side
lengths of a parallelogram equals the sum of the squares of its diagonal lengths.
Angle
The usual notion of an angle is most naturally attached to a real inner product space.
Definition 45 (LADR 6A.16). Let V be a real inner product space. The angle
θ ∈ [0, π] between two nonzero vectors u, v ∈ V is defined by
cos θ =
⟨u, v⟩
∥u∥ ∥v∥
.
The Cauchy–Schwarz inequality guarantees that the quantity on the right lies in
[−1, 1].
For complex inner product spaces, ⟨u, v⟩ need not be real, so this formula does not
directly define an angle without adopting an additional convention.
Example 68. The angle between the vectors (1, 0) and (1, 1) in R
2
satisfies
cos θ =
(1, 0) · (1, 1)
∥(1, 0)∥ ∥(1, 1)∥
=
1
√
2
,
so θ = π/4.
Attention: similarity, retrieval and weighted averaging
Optional application. The remainder of this chapter is not needed for the later
development of the course. It gives a self-contained example of how some of the linear
algebra we have met—linear maps, inner products, matrices and stochastic weighting—
appears inside transformer language models. We will take a little more space than would
usually be available in lecture, because the notation is much easier to understand once
the problem it is designed to address is clear.
132

Language modelling and the problem of context.
A language model assigns probabilities to possible continuations of text. If the text
seen so far is
t1, t2, . . . , ti,
a basic task is to assign probabilities to the token that may come next. A token may be
a word, part of a word or a punctuation mark. Neural language models represent tokens
by vectors and learn how those vector representations should be used in predicting text.
This use of learned vectors predates transformers. In 2003, Bengio, Ducharme,
Vincent and Jauvin proposed a neural probabilistic language model that learned a
distributed vector representation for each word together with a probability model for
word sequences. One motivation was generalization: experience with one sentence
could inform predictions about a new sentence containing words with nearby learned
representations.
A second question is how a model should use information spread through a long sequence. This became especially concrete in neural machine translation. Early encoder–
decoder models compressed a source sentence into a fixed-length vector from which a
decoder generated its translation. In 2014, Bahdanau, Cho and Bengio proposed instead allowing the decoder, while producing a target word, to place different weights on
different parts of the source sentence. They described the mechanism as a “soft-search”
for the source positions most relevant to the next prediction. This is an early form of
the idea now called attention.
Before returning to language, we isolate the mathematical problem in a much simpler
setting.
A soft retrieval problem: addresses and payloads.
Imagine a small memory consisting of m stored items. Associated with item j are
two vectors
(kj, νj ), kj ∈ R
dk
, νj ∈ R
dv
.
We will call kjits key and νjits value. It is helpful at first to think of these as having
quite different jobs:
kj: an address used to decide whether item j should be retrieved,
νj: the payload supplied if item j is retrieved.
The words “address” and “payload” are only an analogy, but they make an important
structural point. The information used to decide which item is relevant need not be
the same as the information we want to obtain from that item. In particular, the key
and value may live in vector spaces of different dimensions.
Now suppose that a request for information is represented by a vector
q ∈ R
dk
,
called a query. The query belongs to the same space as the keys because its first job
is to be compared with them. We use the inner products
⟨q, k1⟩, . . . ,⟨q, km⟩
133

as matching scores. A large score makes the corresponding item influential. For scaled
dot-product attention one sets
sj =
⟨q, kj ⟩
√
dk
and then converts the scores into positive weights by the softmax rule
aj =
e
sj
Xm
ℓ=1
e
sℓ
.
Thus
aj > 0,
Xm
j=1
aj = 1,
and the output is
y =
Xm
j=1
ajνj.
The mechanism therefore separates two operations:
query–key matching decides where to look −→ the values determine what is returned.
The choice is soft rather than all-or-nothing: several stored items may contribute, in
different proportions, to the output.
Example 69 (A deliberately transparent toy memory). Suppose that a tiny memory
is intended to answer three country–capital queries. Its records may be pictured as
France 7−→ Paris, Germany 7−→ Berlin, Italy 7−→ Rome.
For this toy example, we simply choose vector encodings by hand. Represent the three
capital payloads by the standard basis vectors
ν1 = eParis, ν2 = eBerlin, ν3 = eRome
of R
3
, and assign to the three records the keys
k1 = (2, 0), k2 = (0, 1), k3 = (−1, 0).
Let
q = (1, 0)
represent the request for the France record. We are not claiming that these coordinates
are a realistic encoding of countries; they are chosen only so that the roles of query, key
and value can be seen explicitly. Here dk = 2, so the scaled scores are
√

134

Softmax gives weights approximately
0.734, 0.178, 0.088,
and hence

In this toy memory, the first slot is the best match to the query, so its payload dominates
the output. The example is intentionally more human-readable than an actual transformer: in a real model the value vectors are normally internal learned representations,
not literal words or probability vectors over words.
This example also makes clear why a key and its value are paired without being
the same kind of object. The key participates in the matching calculation; the value
is carried along as the payload. Nothing requires the query to resemble the value, and
indeed they need not even have the same dimension.
What does the dot product measure?
For real vectors,
⟨q, k⟩ = ∥q∥ ∥k∥ cos θ.
Thus the score is not simply the angle between the query and key: their lengths matter
as well. If both vectors were normalized to have norm 1, the dot product would become
the cosine similarity cos θ. Attention normally uses the dot product itself.
The factor 1/
√
dk controls the size of the scores. A simple probabilistic heuristic
explains its scale. Suppose, just for this heuristic, that the coordinates qr and kr behave
like independent random variables of mean 0 and variance 1. Then
⟨q, k⟩ =
X
dk
r=1
qrkr
has variance dk. Division by √
dk therefore keeps the typical size of the score of order
one as the dimension grows.
From a hand-designed memory to learned representations.
The preceding memory was artificial: we simply supplied the queries, keys and values. A transformer does something more interesting. At some stage of its computation,
position j in the text has a current internal representation
xj ∈ R
dmodel
.
For the first layer this representation begins from the token representation together
with positional information; in later layers it already contains information produced by
earlier computations. It should therefore be thought of as the model’s current state at
position j, rather than as a human-readable encoding of the token alone.
For one attention head, the model learns three linear maps
WQ, WK : R
dmodel −→ Rdk
, WV : R
dmodel −→ Rdv
.
135

They produce
qi = WQxi, kj = WKxj, νj = WV xj.
The retrieval interpretation now gives these maps distinct functional roles:
WQxi: the matching request made by position i,
WKxj: the matching address offered by position j,
WV xj: the payload position j can contribute.
The equality of the query and key dimensions is structural: the model must be able to
form ⟨qi, kj ⟩. There is no corresponding reason for dv to equal dk.
These descriptions explain the roles of the three vectors, not the meaning of their
individual coordinates. A trained model is not ordinarily supplied with a human interpretation of the key space or the value space. Nor should the value vector be thought
of as being closer to ordinary language than the key. Both are internal learned representations. The key is used for routing—deciding how much information should flow
from one position to another—while the value is the information that is routed. Only
much later, after many layers, is a final representation converted into scores for actual
vocabulary tokens.
Self-attention in a sequence.
We can now return to the sequence
x1, . . . , xm.
The aim is to replace each current representation xi by a new representation that can
incorporate useful information from other positions. Position i forms its query qi and
compares it with every available key kj. Define
aij =
exp ⟨qi, kj ⟩/
√
dk


ℓ=1
exp ⟨qi, kℓ⟩/


.
Then
yi =
Xm
j=1
aijνj.
A useful, but deliberately informal, reading is:
How much should position i listen to position j? is decided by ⟨qi, kj ⟩,
and νj determines what position j contributes when it is listened to. The model is not
given an explicit English-language question at position i; the query vector is simply the
learned representation used for this matching operation.
One can imagine, schematically, that a position whose interpretation depends on
an earlier noun might learn to place substantial weight on a representation carrying
useful information about that noun. This is only an illustration of what the mechanism
136

makes possible. Individual trained attention heads need not admit so simple a linguistic
description.
For a model that generates text from left to right, position i must not use information
from future tokens. The attention is therefore masked: terms corresponding to j > i
are excluded before softmax. The new representation at position i may then combine
information from the available preceding context without seeing text that has not yet
been generated.
For each fixed i,
aij > 0,
X
j
aij = 1.
Thus

is a row-stochastic matrix. This is the row-stochastic convention explicitly noted in
our earlier discussion of Markov chains, where the main convention of these notes was
column-stochastic. Here the ith row has a new interpretation: it records how position
i distributes its attention among the available value vectors.
The matrix calculation.
Put

Its ith column is exactly

Thus, once the attention weights have been determined, the retrieval itself is ordinary
matrix multiplication.
There is a useful comparison with the stochastic matrices encountered earlier:
Markov chain a stochastic matrix moves probability mass,
attention a stochastic matrix mixes retrieved vector information.
The analogy has a limit. In a Markov chain the transition matrix is usually fixed. In
self-attention the matrix A is computed from the current representations, and softmax
is nonlinear. Self-attention as a whole is therefore not a single linear map.
137

A Transformer normally uses several attention heads in parallel and contains many
additional operations. The single-head calculation above isolates the part relevant to
this course. It combines familiar objects in a particular way:
learned linear maps −→ inner-product matching −→ stochastic weighting −→ matrix mixing.
Its main conceptual feature is the separation between routing and payload: query–key
comparisons determine where information is drawn from, while the values determine
what information is passed on.
138

Orthogonality [28–30]
The basic geometric relation in this chapter is perpendicularity, expressed through the
inner product.
Definition 46. Two vectors u, v ∈ V are orthogonal if
⟨u, v⟩ = 0.
Orthogonal vectors are also called perpendicular. Lists of mutually orthogonal
unit vectors are especially useful: coordinates in such a list can be recovered directly by
taking inner products, and norms become sums of squares. Gram–Schmidt provides a
systematic way to construct orthonormal bases, while orthogonal projections decompose
vectors into perpendicular pieces.
The field F will always be either R or C in this chapter.
Orthogonal basis

If V has an orthonormal basis, then it is very easy to find the coefficients of v ∈ V :
Proposition 86 (LADR 6.30). Let e1, ..., en be an orthonormal basis of V . For
any v ∈ V,
v = ⟨v, e1⟩e1 + ... + ⟨v, en⟩en.
Proof. If we write v = a1e1 + ... + anen, then
⟨v, ek⟩ = ⟨a1e1 + ... + anen, ek⟩ = a1⟨e1, ek⟩ + ... + an⟨en, ek⟩ = ak.
Now we prove that every finite-dimensional space has an orthonormal basis. This
proof is algorithmic: the algorithm is called the Gram-Schmidt procedure.

Orthogonal polynomials. The preceding calculation is the beginning of a classical
story. Applying Gram–Schmidt to
1, x, x2, . . .
with the inner product
⟨p, q⟩ =
Z 1
0
p(x)q(x) dx
produces, after normalization, the shifted Legendre polynomials. Using the interval
[−1, 1] gives the usual Legendre polynomials.
Changing the inner product produces other important families. For example, a
Gaussian-weighted inner product on the real line leads to the Hermite polynomials,
which appear naturally in probability as well as analysis. Such classical orthogonal polynomials also arise as eigenfunctions of differential operators. Thus Gram–Schmidt on
a polynomial space provides one small glimpse of the connection among linear algebra,
approximation, probability and differential equations.
Remark: Infinite-dimensional inner product spaces need not have orthogonal Hamel
bases. The reason is that, if {ei}i∈I is an orthogonal basis of V , then every v ∈ V must
be a finite sum v = a1e1 + ... + anen where e1, ..., en ∈ {ei}i∈I , and therefore ⟨v, ei⟩ = 0
for all ei other than e1, ..., en. (In particular, all but finitely many.)
In certain sequence spaces or function spaces, where infinite sums can be defined, an infinite combination of the ei
’s will typically have nonzero scalar products with infinitely
many ei’s, and therefore could not have been a finite sum.
A concrete example of an inner product space with no orthonormal basis is the space
ℓ
2 = {(a1, a2, a3, ...) : X∞
i=1
|ai|
2 < ∞}
142

of sequences whose sum of squares converges, with the inner product
⟨(a1, a2, a3, ...),(b1, b2, b3, ...)⟩ =
X∞
i=1
aibi

Orthogonal complement
Proposition 88 (LADR 6.42, 6.58). Let V be a finite-dimensional inner product
space. Define
ΓV : V −→ V
′
, ΓV (v)(u) = ⟨u, v⟩.
If V is complex, regard both V and V
′ here as real vector spaces. Then ΓV is a
real-linear isomorphism.
When V is a complex vector space, Γ is not complex-linear: because Γ(iv) = −iΓ(v)
instead of Γ(iv) = iΓ(v).
Proof. Γ is real-linear, since ⟨−, −⟩ is real-linear in both components.
It is injective, because: if Γ(v) = 0, then ∥v∥
2 = ⟨v, v⟩ = Γ(v)(v) = 0.
When V is complex, both sides have real dimension 2 dimC V ; in the real case they have
the same ordinary dimension. Thus
dimR V = dimR V
′
,
and injectivity implies that ΓV is a real-linear isomorphism.
In other words, for any functional ψ ∈ V
′
, there is a unique vector v ∈ V with
ψ(u) = ⟨u, v⟩. If we have an orthonormal basis e1, ..., en, then it is easy to find v:
writing
v = a1e1 + ... + anen,
it follows that ψ(ei) = ⟨ei, a1e1 + ... + anen⟩ = ai
; so
v = ψ(e1)e1 + ... + ψ(en)en.
Definition 47 (LADR 6.47). Let U ⊆ V be a subspace. The orthogonal
complement of U is the set
U
⊥ = {v ∈ V : ⟨v, u⟩ = 0 for all u ∈ U}.
U
⊥ itself is a vector subspace: for any v, w ∈ U⊥ and λ ∈ F, and any u ∈ U,
⟨λv + w, u⟩ = λ⟨v, u⟩ + ⟨w, u⟩ = λ · 0 + 0 = 0.
When V is finite-dimensional, the identification Γ : V
∼→ V
′
identifies U
⊥ with U0
,
since the functional ⟨−, v⟩ annihilates U if and only if v ∈ U
⊥.

Definition 48 (LADR 6.55). Let U ⊆ V be a finite-dimensional subspace (so

PU : V −→ V, PU (u + w) = u, u ∈ U, w ∈ U

The direct-sum behaviour that we observed much earlier for projection is in fact
characteristic of every projector.

Proposition 91. Let P ∈ L(V ) satisfy P

Self-adjoint and normal operators [30–31]
The adjoint records how an operator interacts with the inner product. Self-adjoint and
normal operators are the classes for which this interaction has particularly strong consequences: their invariant-subspace structure can be made compatible with orthogonality.
This is the setting in which the spectral theorem will give orthonormal eigenvector decompositions.
Adjoint
In the preceding chapter, we saw that an inner product on a finite-dimensional space
V almost allows you to identify V with its dual V
′ via the real-linear isomorphism
ΓV : V −→ V
′
, ΓV (v) := ⟨−, v⟩
(although multiplication by i did not work out correctly); and under this identification,
the orthogonal complement of a subspace corresponds to its annihilator.
Recall the contrast with the double dual. The canonical map
jV : V −→ V
′′
required no extra structure on V . The identification
ΓV : V −→ V
′
is different: it exists here because an inner product has been chosen, and changing the
inner product changes the identification. This is precisely the distinction we meant
earlier between an isomorphism that merely exists and one that is canonical.
We now study what happens to the dual map.
Definition 49. Let T ∈ L(V, W) be a linear map between finite-dimensional
inner product spaces. The adjoint of T is the map
T
∗
: W −→ V
defined by
⟨T v, w⟩ = ⟨v, T∗w⟩, v ∈ V, w ∈ W.
Other texts use T
†
(“T dagger”) to denote the adjoint.
We are requiring ⟨−, T ∗w⟩ = ⟨−, w⟩ ◦ T = T
′
(⟨−, w⟩); in other words, the diagram

commutes. In particular, T

exists and is unique: it is T
∗ = Γ−1

this with LADR 7A.32.

Self-adjoint operators
Definition 50 (LADR 7.10). Let (V,⟨−, −⟩) be a finite-dimensional inner product space. An operator T ∈ L(V ) is self-adjoint if T = T
∗
.
When V is a real vector space, self-adjoint operators are also called symmetric;
when V is a complex vector space, self-adjoint operators are also called Hermitian.
It is more precise to say that the matrix of a self-adjoint operator with respect to an
orthonormal basis of V is Hermitian; that means, it equals its own conjugate transpose.
Example 74. The matrix 
1 i
−i 1

is Hermitian; on the other hand, the matrix 
1 i
i 1

is not Hermitian.
Proposition 95 (LADR 7.12). Let T ∈ L(V ) be a self-adjoint operator. Then
every eigenvalue of T is real.
This is true by definition when V is a real vector space, but it is an interesting
statement over C.
Proof. Let T v = λv, where v ̸= 0 and λ ∈ F. Then
λ⟨v, v⟩ = ⟨λv, v⟩ = ⟨T v, v⟩ = ⟨v, T v⟩ = ⟨v, λv⟩ = λ⟨v, v⟩,
so λ = λ.
Of course, the converse is false: for example, the matrix 
1 1
0 1
is not Hermitian
(i.e. self-adjoint with respect to the dot product) although it has real eigenvalues. Here
is a sort of converse:
Proposition 96 (LADR 7.14). Let T ∈ L(V ) be an operator on a finitedimensional complex inner product space. Then T is self-adjoint if and only
if
⟨T v, v⟩ ∈ R for every v ∈ V.
Proof. If T is self-adjoint, then
⟨T v, v⟩ = ⟨v, T v⟩ = ⟨T v, v⟩,
so ⟨T v, v⟩ is real.
Conversely, put
Q(x) = ⟨T x, x⟩.
150

Expanding the four terms below gives the polarization identity
⟨T v, w⟩ =
1
4

Q(v + w) − Q(v − w) + iQ(v + iw) − iQ(v − iw)

.
By assumption every value of Q is real. Also Q(−x) = Q(x) and Q(ix) = Q(x).
Applying the displayed identity with v and w interchanged therefore gives
⟨T v, w⟩ = ⟨Tw, v⟩ = ⟨v, Tw⟩.
Thus T = T
∗
.
In particular, the self-adjoint operators as a subset of L(V ) are a kind of generalization of the real numbers R ⊆ L(C).
Proposition 97 (LADR 7.16). Let T ∈ L(V ) be self-adjoint and suppose that
⟨T v, v⟩ = 0 for every v ∈ V.
Then T = 0.
Proof. Put
Q(x) = ⟨T x, x⟩.
If V is real, self-adjointness gives
Q(v + w) − Q(v − w) = 4⟨T v, w⟩.
If V is complex, use the polarization identity from the preceding proof:
⟨T v, w⟩ =
1
4

Q(v + w) − Q(v − w) + iQ(v + iw) − iQ(v − iw)

.
In either case, Q ≡ 0 implies
⟨T v, w⟩ = 0 for all v, w ∈ V.
Taking w = T v gives ∥T v∥
2 = 0, so T v = 0 for every v.

Spectral theorem [31–32]
Real spectral theorem
V will denote a nonzero, finite-dimensional inner product space over R or C.
The spectral theorem is arguably the most important criterion for diagonalizability:
the conditions (self-adjoint resp. normal) are often straightforward to verify, and apply to a large number of operators that occur in “real life”. For example, the Hessian
matrix of a smooth function is symmetric. Also, all rotations and reflections of R
n are
normal.
Proposition 100 (LADR 7.26, 7.27). Let T ∈ L(V ) be a self-adjoint operator.
Then T has an eigenvalue.
Proof. If F = C, existence of an eigenvalue was proved earlier for every operator on
a nonzero finite-dimensional complex vector space. Thus only the real case remains;
assume F = R.
Let p(x) denote the minimal polynomial of T, and assume that p has no real roots.
Then p can be factored in the form
p(x) = (x
2 + b1x + c1) · ... · (x2 + bmx + cm),
where each x
2 + bkx + ck has a pair of complex conjugate roots; in particular, its
discriminant b
2
k − 4ck < 0 is negative. Then T
2 + bkT + ckI is an operator with the
property that, for any nonzero v ∈ V ,
⟨(T
2 + bkT + ckI)v, v⟩ = ⟨T2
v, v⟩ + bk⟨T v, v⟩ + ck⟨v, v⟩
= ∥T v∥
2 + bk⟨T v, v⟩ + ck∥v∥2
,
since ⟨T
2
v, v⟩ = ⟨T v, T v⟩ = ∥T v∥
2 by self-adjointness. Using the Cauchy-Schwarz
153

inequality,
∥T v∥
2 + bk⟨T v, v⟩ + ck∥v∥2 ≥ ∥T v∥2 − |bk|∥T v∥∥v∥ + ck∥v∥2
=

∥T v∥ − 1
2

+

Therefore, (T

and therefore invertible.
But this implies that p(T) = (T
2 + b1T + c1I)· ... ·(T2 + bmT + cmI) is a product of
invertible operators and therefore p(T) is invertible. Contradiction, because p(T) = 0.
Now we prove that self-adjoint operators are semisimple: every invariant subspace
has an invariant complementary subspace. It turns out that over C, this is equivalent
to diagonalizability. It is also the key to the spectral theorem in this situation.
Proposition 101 (LADR 7B.19). Let T ∈ L(V ) be a self-adjoint operator and let
U ⊆ V be an invariant subspace. Then U
⊥ is also invariant, and the restrictions
T|U and T|U⊥ are self-adjoint.
Note that ⟨−, −⟩ restricts to a scalar product on U.
Proof. (i) U
⊥ is invariant, because: for any v ∈ U⊥ and w ∈ U,
⟨T v, w⟩ = ⟨v, Tw⟩ ∈ ⟨v, U⟩ = {0},
so T v is also orthogonal to U.
(ii) T|U is self-adjoint, because
⟨T|U v, w⟩ = ⟨T v, w⟩ = ⟨v, Tw⟩ = ⟨v, T|U w⟩
for any v, w ∈ U. Similarly, T|U⊥ is also self-adjoint.
Proposition 102 (LADR 7.29). Let T ∈ L(V ) be an operator.
(i) Any self-adjoint operator T is orthogonally diagonalizable: there is an
orthonormal basis of V consisting of eigenvectors of T.
(ii) If V is a real vector space, then any orthogonally diagonalizable operator is
self-adjoint.
154

Proof. (i) Induction on n = dim(V ). This is clear when n = 1.
In general, fix an eigenvector v1 ∈ V and assume without loss of generality that
∥v1∥ = 1. Define U := Span(v1); then U is an invariant subspace, so U
⊥ is also invariant. By induction, T|U⊥ is orthogonally diagonalizable: there is an orthonormal basis
v2, ..., vn of U
⊥ consisting of eigenvectors of T|U⊥ . Then v1, ..., vn is an orthonormal
basis of V consisting of eigenvectors of T.
(ii) Let v1, ..., vn be an orthonormal basis of V consisting of eigenvectors of T, and
let λ1, ..., λn be the corresponding eigenvalues. Then, for any i, j,
⟨T vi, vj ⟩ = ⟨λivi, vj ⟩ = λiδij = λjδij = ⟨vi, λjvj ⟩ = ⟨vi, T vj ⟩.
This implies that ⟨T v, w⟩ = ⟨v, Tw⟩ for any vectors v, w ∈ V . Here, we are using the
fact that λjis real in order to know that ⟨vi, λjvj ⟩ = λj ⟨vi, vj ⟩.
A probabilistic connection. Finite reversible Markov chains provide an important
setting in which the spectral theorem enters probability. With an appropriate weighted
inner product determined by the stationary distribution, the transition operator is selfadjoint. Its eigenvalues are therefore real and it has an orthonormal eigenbasis. In
this setting the eigenvalue 1 represents equilibrium, while the remaining eigenvalues
describe how deviations from equilibrium decay. The spectral gap is closely connected
with bottlenecks and with the speed of convergence to equilibrium.
There is a related graph-theoretic example. The adjacency matrix of an undirected
graph is symmetric, so the real spectral theorem gives it an orthonormal eigenbasis.
This observation is a starting point for spectral graph theory.
Complex spectral theorem
V will denote a nonzero, finite-dimensional inner product space over C.
The following theorem comprises the main part of the complex spectral theorem.
We will give several independent proofs to make the theorem extra convincing.
Proposition 103. Let T ∈ L(V ) be a normal operator. Then T is diagonalizable.
Proof. Let p(x) be the minimal polynomial of T, and assume that p has a double root:
i.e. p(x) = (x−λ)
2
r(x) for some λ ∈ F and r ∈ P(C). Define q(x) := (x−λ)r(x) = p(x)
x−λ
.
Then p is a factor of q
2
, so q(T)
2 = 0. Moreover q(T) is normal: because T commutes
with T
∗

commutes with q(T)
∗

0 = ∥q(T)q(T)v∥

q(T)v∥
2
for all v ∈ V,

2
for all v ∈ V, so q(T) = 0. This is a contradiction, because deg(q) < deg(p).
Proof. Consider the decomposition T = R + iS, where R, S are self-adjoint and
RS = SR. By the real spectral theorem, R and S are both diagonalizable; since they
commute, the sum R + iS is also diagonalizable.
Explicitly: each eigenspace E(λ, S) of S is R-invariant, because Sv = λv implies
S(Rv) = RSv = R(λv) = λRv. By diagonalizing R|E(λ,S)for each eigenvalue λ of S,
we get a basis of V consisting of vectors that are simultaneously eigenvectors for R and
for S. Any such vector will also be an eigenvector of R + iS.
Proof. Induction on n = dim(V ). When n = 1, this is clear.
In general, let λ be any eigenvalue of T, and define the T-invariant subspace
U = range(T − λI). Then U is also T

-invariant, because:

|U . By induction, U admits a basis of eigenvectors of T. Also, since T is
normal, the orthogonal complement of U is
U
⊥ = range(T − λI)⊥ = null(T − λI),
which admits a basis of eigenvectors of T by definition. Therefore, V admits a basis of
eigenvectors of T.
Proof. Let v1, ..., vk be any Jordan chain for T for some eigenvalue λ ∈ C, and assume
that k ≥ 2. In other words, T vj = λvj + vj−1 for all j. Then
v1 = (T − λI)v2,
so
v1 ∈ range(T − λI) ∩ null(T − λI).
Since T − λI is normal, it follows that null(T − λI) = range(T − λI)
⊥ and therefore
v1 = 0; contradiction.
Therefore, the Jordan normal form of T consists only of (1 × 1)-blocks; i.e. T is
diagonalizable.

Proof. Schur’s theorem states that, for any operator T, there is an orthonormal

2 = ∥T e1∥2 = ∥T∗

2 = |a1,1|2 + |a1,2|2 + ... + |a1,n|2
,
so a1,2 = ... = a1,n = 0. It follows that
|a2,2|
2 = |a1,2|2 + |a2,2|2 = ∥T e2∥2 = ∥T∗
e2∥
2 = |a2,2|2 + |a2,3|2 + ... + |a2,n|2
,
so a2,3 = ... = a2,n = 0.
This argument shows that all nondiagonal entries of M(T) are 0, so e1, ..., en is a basis
of eigenvectors of T.
Now we prove the spectral theorem:
Proposition 104 (LADR 7.31). Let T ∈ L(V ) be an operator. The following
are equivalent:
(i) T is normal;
(ii) T is orthogonally diagonalizable.
Proof. (i) ⇒ (ii): We have already seen that T is diagonalizable. If v, w are eigenvectors
for distinct eigenvalues of T, with T v = λv and Tw = µw, then
w = (T − λI)(µ − λ)
−1w ∈ range(T − λI) = null(T − λI)⊥
and therefore ⟨v, w⟩ = 0.
(ii) ⇒ (i): Let v1, ..., vn be an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors of T with eigenvalues
λ1, ..., λn. Then
⟨T
∗T vi
, vj ⟩ = ⟨T vi, T vj ⟩ = ⟨λivi, λjvj ⟩ = λiλjδij = |λi|
2
δij ,
and
⟨T T∗vi, vj ⟩ = ⟨T
∗
vi, T∗vj ⟩ = ⟨λivi
, λjvj ⟩ = λiλjδij = |λi|
2
δij ;
these are equal, so T
∗T = T T∗
.

Positive operators and isometries [32–33]
The spectral theorem lets us read many properties of a self-adjoint operator from its
eigenvalues. Positive operators are those whose eigenvalues are nonnegative; they admit
positive square roots. These square roots will soon be used to separate the stretching
part of a general operator from its isometric part.
Positive operators
Definition 52. Let V be a finite-dimensional inner product space. A self-adjoint
operator T ∈ L(V ) is positive if
⟨T v, v⟩ ≥ 0
for all v ∈ V.
Positive operators are often called positive semidefinite. This is distinguished
from positive definite operators, which have the strict inequality: ⟨T v, v⟩ > 0 for all
v ̸= 0.
Example 75. The orthogonal projection PU onto a subspace U ⊆ V is positive semidefinite, because: for any v ∈ V ,
⟨PU (v), v⟩ = ⟨PU (v), PU (v) + v − PU (v)
| {z }
∈U⊥
⟩ = ⟨PU (v), PU (v)⟩ ≥ 0.
Proposition 105 (LADR 7.38). Let T ∈ L(V ) be an operator on a finitedimensional inner product space. The following are equivalent:
(i) T is positive semidefinite;
(ii) T is self-adjoint, and all eigenvalues of T are nonnegative;
(iii) T is the square of a self-adjoint operator;
(iv) There is an operator R ∈ L(V ) such that T = R∗R.
Proof. (i) ⇒ (ii): Self-adjointness is part of the definition of a positive operator. Let v
be an eigenvector of T with eigenvalue λ; then λ∥v∥
2 = ⟨λv, v⟩ = ⟨T v, v⟩ ≥ 0, so λ ≥ 0.
158

(ii) ⇒ (iii): Let e1, ..., en be an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors of T, with real nonnegative eigenvalues λ1, ..., λn (which exists by the spectral theorem). Define an operator
R by Rek =
√
λkek. Then R is orthogonally diagonalizable with real eigenvalues, so it
is self-adjoint; and R2ek =
√
λk
2
ek = λkek = T ek for all k, so R2 = T.
(iii) ⇒ (iv): If T = R2 where R is self-adjoint, then T is also R∗R.
(iv) ⇒ (i): For any v ∈ V ,
⟨T v, v⟩ = ⟨R
∗Rv, v⟩ = ⟨Rv, Rv⟩ ≥ 0.
In particular, knowing that (ii) ⇒ (i) holds, we see that the operator R constructed
in the proof of (ii) ⇒ (iii) is positive semidefinite; so every positive semidefinite operator
has a positive semidefinite square root.
There is a similar characterization of positive definite operators:
Proposition 106. Let T ∈ L(V ) be an operator on a finite-dimensional inner
product space. The following are equivalent:
(i) T is positive definite;
(ii) T is self-adjoint, and all eigenvalues of T are strictly positive;
(iii) There is an invertible operator R ∈ L(V ) such that T = R∗R.
The proof is almost exactly the same. You should work out the details!
Finally, we will show that the positive square root is unique:
Proposition 107 (LADR 7.39, 7.40). Let T ∈ L(V ) be a positive semidefinite
operator. Then the positive square root is unique. It is denoted √T .
Example 76. An operator can have many square roots if positivity is not required.
For example, the identity on C
2 has infinitely many square roots: for any a, b ∈ C with
b ̸= 0,
R =

a b
(1 − a
2
)/b −a

satisfies
R
2 = I.
Thus uniqueness is a special feature of the positive square root.
Proof. (i) First suppose T = λI with λ ≥ 0. If R is positive and R2 = λI, then every
eigenvalue µ of R is nonnegative and satisfies
µ
2 = λ.
159

Thus every eigenvalue of R equals √
λ. Because R is diagonalizable, this forces
R =
√
λ I.
(ii) Now let T be any positive operator and let R be a positive square root of T.
Since R2 = T, the operators R and T commute. Hence every eigenspace E(λ, T) is
invariant under R. On this eigenspace the restriction of R is a positive square root of
T|E(λ,T) = λI.
By part (i),
R|E(λ,T) =
√
λ I.
The eigenspaces of T span V by the spectral theorem, so these formulas determine R
uniquely.
One of the important applications of positive operators is in classifying the inner
products on R
n
(or C
n
):
Proposition 108. Every inner product on C
n has the form
⟨v, w⟩ = v
TGw
for a unique Hermitian positive-definite matrix G. Conversely, every Hermitian
positive-definite matrix G defines an inner product by this formula.
Proof. Let e1, . . . , en be the standard basis and define
Gij = ⟨ei, ej ⟩.
If v =
P
i

P

, sesquilinearity gives
⟨v, w⟩ =
X

Thus G is uniquely determined by the inner product. Conjugate symmetry gives
Gji = Gij ,
so G is Hermitian, and positive definiteness gives
v
TGv > 0 (v ̸= 0).
Conversely, suppose G is Hermitian and v
TGv > 0 for every nonzero v. The formula
⟨v, w⟩ = v
TGw
160

is linear in v and conjugate-linear in w. Because G = G
T
,
⟨v, w⟩ = v
TG w = wTGv = ⟨w, v⟩.
The assumed inequality gives positive definiteness. Hence the formula defines an inner
product.
Covariance matrices and principal directions
Positive operators arise naturally in probability and statistics. Let X be a random
vector in R
n with finite second moments, and put
m = EX.
Its covariance matrix is
Σ = E

(X − m)(X − m)
T

.
The matrix Σ is symmetric, and for every v ∈ R
n
,
v
TΣv = E

(v
T
(X − m))2

≥ 0.
Thus covariance matrices are positive semidefinite.
The spectral theorem therefore gives an orthonormal basis of eigenvectors of Σ. For
a unit vector v, the quantity
v
TΣv = Var(vT X)
is the variance of the data seen in the direction v. The eigenvector corresponding to the
largest eigenvalue is a direction of maximal variance; successive orthogonal eigenvectors
give the further principal directions. This is the linear-algebraic core of principal
component analysis (PCA). Equivalently, these directions can be obtained from
the singular value decomposition of a centered data matrix, which gives another link
to the material in the next chapter.
Isometries
Definition 53 (LADR 7.44). A linear map S ∈ L(V, W) between inner product
spaces is an isometry if ∥Sv∥ = ∥v∥ for all v ∈ V.
This differs from the definition in the textbook, which only refers to operators on a
single space as isometries.
Isometric operators on real inner product spaces are usually called orthogonal operators; isometric operators on complex inner product spaces are usually called unitary
operators.
161

Proposition 109 (LADR 7.49). Let S ∈ L(V, W) be a linear map between finitedimensional inner product spaces. The following are equivalent:
(i) S is an isometry;
(ii) S
∗S = I.
Proof. (i) ⇒ (ii): Assume that ∥Sv∥ = ∥v∥ for all v. Then
⟨S
∗Sv, v⟩ = ⟨Sv, Sv⟩ = ⟨v, v⟩ for all v ∈ V,
so S
∗S − I is a self-adjoint operator with the property that
⟨(S
∗S − I)v, v⟩ = 0
for all v ∈ V. This implies that S
∗S − I = 0, so S∗S = I.
(ii) ⇒ (i): If S
∗S = I, then
∥Sv∥
2 = ⟨Sv, Sv⟩ = ⟨S∗Sv, v⟩ = ⟨v, v⟩ = ∥v∥2
.
Taking square roots gives ∥Sv∥ = ∥v∥ for every v ∈ V .
In particular, every isometric operator is normal: its adjoint is its inverse. Permutation matrices give elementary examples of isometries on R
n or Cn
: they merely
rearrange the coordinates and therefore preserve the standard Euclidean norm.
Remark: The adjoint of any isometric operator is also isometric: this is because
S
∗S = I implies that S∗ = S−1
, so (S
∗
)
∗S∗ = SS∗ = SS−1 = I. On Rn with the
dot product, this reduces to the statement that, if the columns of a square matrix are
orthonormal, then the rows of that matrix are also orthonormal.
The adjoint of an isometry between two different spaces does not need to be isometric.
Proposition 110 (LADR 7.49). Let S ∈ L(V, W) be a linear map. The following
are equivalent:
(i) S is an isometry;
(ii) ⟨Su, Sv⟩ = ⟨u, v⟩ for all u, v ∈ V ;
(iii) For any orthonormal basis e1, ..., en of V , S(e1), ..., S(en) is an orthonormal
list in W;
(iv) There is an orthonormal basis e1, ..., en of V such that S(e1), ..., S(en) is an
orthonormal list in W.
Proof. (i) ⇒ (ii) Since S
∗S = I, it follows that ⟨Su, Sv⟩ = ⟨S∗Su, v⟩ = ⟨u, v⟩ for all
v ∈ V.
(ii) ⇒ (iii): This is because ⟨S(ei), S(ej )⟩ = ⟨ei, ej ⟩ = δij for all i, j.
(iii) ⇒ (iv): This is clear.
162

(iv) ⇒ (i): For any v = λ1e1 + ... + λnen, by the generalized Pythagorean theorem,
∥Sv∥
2 = ∥λ1S(e1) + ... + λnS(en)∥2 = |λ1|2 + ... + |λn|2 = ∥λ1e1 + ... + λnen∥2 = ∥v∥2
,
so ∥Sv∥ = ∥v∥.
Proposition 111 (LADR 7.55). Let S ∈ L(V ) be an operator on a finitedimensional complex inner product space. The following are equivalent:
(i) S is an isometry;
(ii) S is normal, and the absolute value of any eigenvalue of S is 1.
Proof. (i) ⇒ (ii): Every isometry S commutes with its adjoint S
∗ = S−1
. Also, if λ
is an eigenvalue of S with eigenvector v, then ∥v∥ = ∥Sv∥ = ∥λv∥ = |λ|· ∥v∥, so |λ| = 1.
(ii) ⇒ (i): Since S is normal, if λ is any eigenvalue of S with eigenvector v,
S
∗Sv = S∗
(λv) = λS∗v = λλv = |λ|
2
v = v.
Therefore, S
∗S agrees with I on a basis of eigenvectors of S, so S∗S = I.

Polar and singular value decomposition [33–35]
We now return to an arbitrary linear operator on an inner product space. The positive
operator T
∗T measures how much T stretches each direction. Polar decomposition
separates this change of size from an isometry, and the singular value decomposition
refines the picture into orthonormal input and output directions on which the stretching
is simply multiplication by nonnegative numbers.
This will also give a precise way to identify the most important directions of a large
linear map and to form optimal low-rank approximations.
Polar decomposition
Proposition 112 (LADR 7.93). Let T ∈ L(V ) be an operator on a finitedimensional inner product space. Then there is an isometry S such that
T = S
√
T
∗T .
Here, √T
∗T is the positive semidefinite square root of T
∗T.
Proof. We define the linear map

Singular value decomposition
Singular value decompositions of a matrix (or operator) are used in applied mathematics
and statistics. Instead of studying the operator T itself, we study the eigenvalues of
the positive semidefinite operator √
T
∗T; the spectral theorem guarantees that √
T
∗T
will have nice properties (for example, diagonalizable with real eigenvalues), and we use
this to approximate properties of T.
Although we will not go into the applications here, you may remember a similar idea
from Math 54: to approximate solutions to an unsolvable system of equations Ax = b,
we studied the normal equations (ATA)x = ATb instead.
Definition 54 (LADR 7.65). Let T ∈ L(V ). The singular values of T are the
nonnegative square roots of the eigenvalues of T
∗T, counted with multiplicity.
Equivalently, they are the eigenvalues of √
T
∗T.
Since √T
∗T is positive, the singular values s1, ..., sn of T are all real and nonnegative.
Remark: The greatest singular value of T is often called the spectral norm of T,
denoted ∥T∥2.
This is definite, because: if all eigenvalues of √
T
∗T are 0, then it follows that √
T
∗T = 0;
using the polar decomposition, we conclude that T = 0.
It is homogeneous, because: for any λ ∈ C, the eigenvalues of p
(λT)
∗
(λT) = |λ|
√
T
∗T
are exactly |λ| times the eigenvalues of √T
∗T .
The triangle inequality is harder to verify.
Question for the reader: is the spectral norm induced by an inner product on L(V )?
Proposition 113 (LADR 7.70). Let T ∈ L(V ) have singular values s1, ..., sn.
Then there are orthonormal bases e1, ..., en and f1, ..., fn of V such that
T v = s1⟨v, e1⟩f1 + ... + sn⟨v, en⟩fn

Order the singular values so that

The preceding formula gives a useful characterization of the spectral norm.

Low-rank approximation
One of the most important uses of the singular value decomposition is to replace a
complicated linear map by a lower-rank map that retains its most significant directions.
Order the singular values so that
s1 ≥ s2 ≥ · · · ≥ sn ≥ 0.
From the singular value decomposition,
T v =
Xn
j=1
sj ⟨v, ej ⟩fj.
For 1 ≤ k < n, define
Tkv =
X
k
j=1
sj ⟨v, ej ⟩fj.
Then
range(Tk) ⊆ Span(f1, . . . , fk),
so
rank(Tk) ≤ k.
In other words, Tk keeps the first k singular directions and discards the rest.
How good is this approximation?
Proposition 115 (Best low-rank approximation). Let T have singular values
s1 ≥ · · · ≥ sn ≥ 0.
For k < n, the operator Tk defined above satisfies
∥T − Tk∥2 = sk+1.
Moreover, if R is any linear map with
rank(R) ≤ k,
then
∥T − R∥2 ≥ sk+1.
Thus Tk is a best approximation to T, in spectral norm, among all operators of
rank at most k.
Proof. For
v =
Xn
j=1
cjej,

we have

Xn

Because the fj are orthonormal,

|cj|

k+1 Xn

Thus

Taking v = ek+1 gives equality:
∥(T − Tk)ek+1∥ = sk+1.
Hence
∥T − Tk∥2 = sk+1.
Now let R have rank at most k, and consider the (k + 1)-dimensional subspace
U = Span(e1, . . . , ek+1).
The restriction
R|U : U −→ range(R)
maps a (k + 1)-dimensional space into a space of dimension at most k. Therefore R|U
cannot be injective. Choose a unit vector

Thus no rank-k operator can approximate T more closely, in spectral norm, than Tk.
Notice the return of our finite-dimensional “pigeonhole principle”. A map whose
169

range has dimension at most k cannot distinguish all directions in the (k+1)-dimensional
space
Span(e1, . . . , ek+1);
some nonzero direction must be sent to 0. This simple dimension argument is exactly
what makes the lower bound work.
Large matrices and low-rank models.
This theorem helps explain why the singular value decomposition is useful when
matrices become very large. If the singular values
s1, s2, . . .
decrease rapidly, then a map of large rank may be well approximated by Tk for a much
smaller value of k.
The formula
Tkv =
X
k
j=1
sj ⟨v, ej ⟩fj
shows that only k input directions and k output directions are needed to describe the
approximation. In matrix language this is the factorization
Ak = UkΣkV
∗
k
,
where V
∗
k denotes the conjugate transpose (ordinary transpose over R), and the middle
matrix has only k nonzero singular values.
This idea is relevant whenever very large learned linear maps occur. Large language models, for example, contain many matrices acting on high-dimensional vector
representations. Replacing, approximating, or modifying such a matrix through lowerrank factors can greatly reduce the number of parameters that need to be stored or
manipulated.
Thus the singular value decomposition gives a precise answer to a natural question:
if only k independent directions may be retained, which k directions should we keep?
In spectral norm, the answer is: keep the directions corresponding to the k largest
singular values.
This can be formulated as follows: the matrix of T with respect to the orthonormal
bases B = (e1, ..., en) and C = (f1, ..., fn) is
MB
C
(T) =


s1 0
.
.
.
0 sn

 .
This property determines the singular values:
Proposition 116. Assume that e1, ..., en and f1, ..., fn are orthonormal bases of
V with the property that T ek = skfk for nonnegative real numbers sk. Then
s1, ..., sn are the singular values of T.

Proof. Since

of √

Complexification [35–36]
Complexifying vector spaces and operators
Complexifying a real vector space is an abstraction of creating C
n
from R
n by attaching
imaginary parts to real vectors.
Definition 55 (LADR 1B.8). Let V be a real vector space. The complexification
of V is the set
VC = V × V,
with its componentwise addition. Scalar multiplication by complex numbers is
defined by
(a + bi) · (v, w) := (av − bw, bv + aw).
This makes VC a C-vector space. Elements (v, w) ∈ VC are usually denoted v + iw.
Proposition 117 (LADR 2B.11). Let V be a real vector space with basis v1, ..., vn.
Then v1, ..., vn are a basis of VC.
Here, vk denotes vk + 0i ∈ VC.
Proof. (i) v1, ..., vn spans VC, because: let v + iw ∈ VC be any element, and write
v = λ1v1 + ... + λnvn, w = µ1v1 + ... + µnvn, λk, µk ∈ R.
Then v + iw = (λ1 + iµ1)v1 + ... + (λn + iµn)vn.
(ii) v1, ..., vn is linearly independent, because: assume that
(λ1 + iµ1)v1 + ... + (λn + iµn)vn = 0.
Comparing the real and imaginary parts shows that
λ1v1 + ... + λnvn = µ1v1 + ... + µnvn = 0
in the real vector space V , so λ1 = µ1 = ... = λn = µn = 0.
173

Definition 56 (LADR 3B.33). Let V be a real vector space and let T ∈ L(V )
be an operator. The complexification of T is the map
TC : VC −→ VC, TC(v + iw) = T(v) + iT(w).
TC is C-linear, because: it is R-linear, and
TC(i(v + iw)) = TC(iv − w) = T(−w) + iT(v) = i(T(v) + iT(w)) = iTC(v + iw)
for all v, w ∈ V.
Proposition 118. Let T ∈ L(V ) be an operator on a real vector space, and fix
a basis v1, ..., vn. Then the matrix M(T) of T equals the matrix M(TC) of TC.
In particular, the complexification of a matrix map A ∈ R
n,n is just the same matrix,
where the entries are interpreted as complex numbers: A ∈ C
n,n
.
Proof. If M(T) = (aij )i,j , then T vj =
Pn
i=1 aijvi
; therefore,
TC(vj ) = T(vj ) = Xn
i=1
aijvi,
so M(TC) = (aij )i,j .
Algebraic information under complexification
TC inherits many of the properties of T. We will list a few here.
Proposition 119 (LADR 5B.27). Let T ∈ L(V ) be an operator on a real vector
space. Then the minimal polynomial of T equals the minimal polynomial of TC.
Proof. Using the definition TC(u + iv) = T(u) + iT(v), it follows that
T
2

then we see that
p(TC)(u + iv) = p(T)u + ip(T)v = 0 + 0i.

Proposition 120. Let T ∈ L(V ) be an operator on a real vector space. A real
number λ ∈ R is an eigenvalue of T if and only if it is an eigenvalue of TC, and
E(λ, TC) = E(λ, T)C, G(λ, TC) = G(λ, T)C.
Proof. We first show that null(TC) = null(T)C; i.e. the null space of TC is the complexification of the null space of T. This is because
u + iv ∈ null(TC) ⇔ T u + iT v = 0 + 0i ⇔ u, v ∈ null(T).

Conjugation and real structure
It will be useful to have a notion of taking the complex conjugate of an operator. The
idea is that, for matrices, the complex conjugate of a matrix should just consist of the
complex conjugate in each entry.
175

For v + iw ∈ VC define
v + iw = v − iw.
For S ∈ L(VC), define its conjugate operator by
S(x) = S(x).
Although vector conjugation is conjugate-linear, the two conjugations in this formula
cancel, so S is complex-linear. Indeed, for α ∈ C,
S(αx) = S(α x) = α S(x) = α S(x).
We can check the following properties:
(i) Sv = Sv for all v ∈ VC, by definition;
(ii) S + T = S + T for all S, T ∈ L(VC);
(iii) for any S, T ∈ L(VC) and v ∈ VC,
S · T v = ST · v = STv = (ST)v = ST v,
so ST = S · T .
Proposition 121. Let S ∈ L(VC) be an operator on the complexification of a
real vector space V . Then S = TC for some T ∈ L(V ) if and only if S = S.
Proof. Assume that S = S. Then, for any v ∈ V,
Sv = Sv = Sv = Sv.
Therefore, S defines an operator T : V → V , T v + 0i := S(v + 0i). Then S = TC,
because: for any v + iw ∈ VC,
S(v + iw) = Sv + iSw = T v + iTw = TC(v + iw).
On the other hand, the conjugate of any operator TC is
TC(v + iw) = TC(v − iw) = T(v) − iT(w) = T(v) + iT(w) = TC(v + iw),
i.e. TC = TC.
Passing to TC can create new (nonreal) eigenvalues. The eigenspace of any nonreal
eigenvalue and its complex conjugate are closely related:
Proposition 122. Let T ∈ L(V ) be an operator on a real vector space and λ ∈ C.
For any k ∈ N and u, v ∈ V,
(TC − λI)
k
(u + iv) = 0 ⇔ (TC − λI)
k
(u − iv) = 0.
176

Proof. This is because
(TC − λI)
k
(u − iv) = (TC − λI)
k
(u + iv).
In particular, the complex conjugation is an R-linear map between G(λ, TC) and
G(λ, TC), so the algebraic multiplicities of λ and λ are equal.
Definition 57. Let T ∈ L(V ) be an operator on a real vector space. The
characteristic polynomial of T is defined as the characteristic polynomial of
its complexification TC.
Once determinant has been defined, this polynomial can also be computed from any
real representing matrix A of T as
det(xI − A).
The Cayley-Hamilton theorem holds: any operator T satisfies its characteristic equation, because TC satisfies its characteristic equation.
177

Normal operators on a real space [36–37]
Real normal operators
Given a real inner product space V , the complexification becomes an inner product
space via
⟨v1 + iw1, v2 + iw2⟩ := ⟨v1, v2⟩ + ⟨w1, w2⟩ + i

⟨w1, v2⟩ − ⟨v1, w2⟩

.
The inner-product axioms follow directly from the definition.
Proposition 123. Let T ∈ L(V ) be an operator. Then:
(i) T is self-adjoint if and only if TC is self-adjoint;
(ii) T is normal if and only if TC is normal.
Proof. This will be clear when we show that the complexification of T
∗
is always (TC)
∗
.
For any v1, v2, w1, w2 ∈ V,
⟨TC(v1 + iw1), v2 + iw2⟩ = ⟨T(v1) + iT(w1), v2 + iw2⟩

The eigenvalues of the complexified operator TC are either real or come in complex

Proposition 126. Let V be a real inner product space and T ∈ L(V ). Then T
is normal if and only if there is an orthonormal basis of V , with respect to which
T is represented by a block diagonal matrix of (1 × 1)-blocks and (2 × 2)-blocks of
the form 
a −b
b a 
with b > 0.
Proof. Each indicated block is normal, so any block-diagonal matrix of this form is
normal.
Assume that T is normal. We use induction on dim(V ).
(i) If dim(V ) = 1, then T is represented by a (1 × 1)-block.
(ii) If dim(V ) = 2, then T is either self-adjoint (in which case it is orthogonally
diagonalizable; i.e. we get two (1 × 1)-blocks) or it is represented by a matrix of the
form 
a −b
b a 
with b > 0.
(iii) If dim(V ) ≥ 3, then we can find a proper invariant subspace U: if T has a real
180

eigenvalue, then we can let U be the span of an eigenvector. Otherwise, if TC has a
complex eigenvalue (a + ib) with complex eigenvector u + iv, then
T(u) + iT(v) = TC(u + iv) = (a + ib)(u + iv) = (au − bv) + i(bu + av).
Because b ̸= 0, the real vectors u and v are linearly independent (otherwise the eigenvector would be a complex multiple of a real eigenvector, forcing a real eigenvalue).
Thus
U = Span(u, v)
is a two-dimensional invariant subspace. Since T|U and T|U⊥ are normal, they can both
be represented by matrices of this form; therefore, T can be represented by a matrix of
this form.
Real isometries
We proved earlier that (T
∗
)C = (TC)
∗
for any operator T ∈ L(V ) on a real inner product
space. In particular, T is an isometry if and only if TC is an isometry.
Proposition 127. Let S ∈ L(V ) be an operator on a finite-dimensional real
inner product space. The following are equivalent:
(i) S is an isometry;
(ii) There is an orthonormal basis of V , with respect to which S is represented by
a block-diagonal matrix consisting of (1 × 1)-blocks ±1 and (2 × 2)-blocks of the
form

cos(θ) − sin(θ)
sin(θ) cos(θ)

, θ ∈ (0, π).
Thus a real isometry decomposes into rotations on mutually orthogonal two-dimensional
planes together with sign changes on mutually orthogonal one-dimensional subspaces.
Proof. Any isometry S is normal (it commutes with its adjoint S
∗ = S−1
), so it
has a representation with respect to an orthonormal basis by a matrix consisting
of (1 × 1)-blocks and (2 × 2)-blocks of the form 
a −b
b a 
, with b > 0. Also, the
complexification SC is an isometry on VC, so its eigenvalues all have absolute value
1. This forces the (1 × 1)-blocks to be ±1, and the (2 × 2)-blocks 
a −b
b a 
have
eigenvalues a ± ib, so a

some θ ∈ (0, π).
On the other hand, the blocks (±1) and 
a −b
b a 
have inverse equal to their trans181

pose, so this is true for the entire matrix of S; therefore, S is represented by an isometry
with respect to an orthonormal basis of V , so S itself is an isometry.

Determinant [37–40]
The determinant has already appeared in these notes as a deliberately small computational tool. Near the beginning of the course we used only the 2 × 2 formula ad − bc
to test invertibility, and later used det(tI − A) to calculate characteristic polynomials
in small examples. When the general matrix determinant was mentioned, we treated
its familiar properties as material recalled from Math 54 rather than developing them
here.
We now develop determinant systematically and connect that calculation with several structures developed along the way. The determinant will appear as the scalar
action of an operator on top-degree alternating forms, as the product of its eigenvalues,
and–through the singular value decomposition–as a volume-scaling factor. Thus this
final topic brings together coordinates, invariant structure and geometry in one scalar
quantity.
Top-degree alternating forms
Let V be a finite-dimensional vector space and put
n = dim(V ).
We will use one small piece of multilinear algebra. An alternating n-linear form on
V is a function
ω : V
n −→ F
that is linear in each variable and satisfies
ω(v1, . . . , vn) = 0
whenever two of the vectors v1, . . . , vn are equal. We denote the vector space of such
forms by
Altn(V ).
Example 79. For V = R
2
, the familiar formula
ω
a1
b1

,

a2
b2
 = a1b2 − a2b1
defines an alternating 2-linear form. This is the usual determinant of the matrix whose
columns are the two vectors.
183

Alternation implies the familiar sign change under interchange of two arguments.
Proposition 128 (LADR 9.30). Let ω ∈ Altn(V ). If two entries in
ω(v1, . . . , vn)
are interchanged, then the value is multiplied by −1.
Proof. It is enough to consider two slots containing v and w. Put v + w in both slots.
Alternation gives
0 = ω(. . . , v + w, . . . , v + w, . . .).
After expanding by multilinearity, the terms containing two copies of v or two copies
of w vanish, leaving
ω(. . . , v, . . . , w, . . .) + ω(. . . , w, . . . , v, . . .) = 0.
This gives the result.
The feature of top-degree alternating forms that we need is the following.
Proposition 129 (LADR 9.37). If dim(V ) = n, then
dim Altn(V ) = 1.
Proof. Choose a basis
e1, . . . , en
of V . First we show that an alternating n-linear form is completely determined by its
value on this basis.
For v1, . . . , vn ∈ V , write
vj =
Xn
i=1
aijei.
Expanding
ω(v1, . . . , vn)
by multilinearity gives a sum of terms of the form
ai11 · · · ainnω(ei1, . . . , ein).
If two of the indices i1, . . . , in are equal, the corresponding term vanishes by alternation.
Thus every surviving term contains each of
e1, . . . , en
184

exactly once. By repeated interchange of entries, its value is therefore either
ω(e1, . . . , en)
or its negative. Hence the single scalar
ω(e1, . . . , en)
determines ω. This shows that
dim Altn(V ) ≤ 1.
It remains to see that a nonzero alternating n-linear form exists. For this existence
step, use the usual determinant of an n-by-n matrix, familiar from Math 54. Define
ωB(v1, . . . , vn) = det


| |
[v1]B · · · [vn]B
| |

 ,
where B = (e1, . . . , en). The usual multilinearity and alternation properties of the
matrix determinant show that
ωB ∈ Altn(V ),
and
ωB(e1, . . . , en) = 1.
Thus Altn(V ) contains a nonzero element, and so it has dimension one.
The matrix determinant in the last proof is being used only as a familiar coordinate
calculation. We now define the determinant of an abstract operator without choosing
coordinates. Later we will see that the two notions agree.
The determinant of an operator
Let T ∈ L(V ). If
ω ∈ Altn(V ),
define another n-linear form by
ωT (v1, . . . , vn) = ω(T v1, . . . , T vn).
Because T is linear, ωT is multilinear; and if two of the vj are equal, then the corresponding two vectors T vj are equal. Thus ωT is alternating.
Consequently T induces a linear operator
ΦT : Altn(V ) −→ Altn(V ), ΦT (ω) = ωT .
185

The space on which ΦT acts is one-dimensional. Every operator on a one-dimensional
vector space is multiplication by a unique scalar.
Definition 58 (LADR 9.41). Let T ∈ L(V ), where dim(V ) = n. The determinant of T, denoted det(T), is the unique scalar such that
ωT = (det T)ω for every ω ∈ Altn(V ).
Equivalently,
ω(T v1, . . . , T vn) = (det T) ω(v1, . . . , vn)
for every alternating n-linear form ω and every v1, . . . , vn ∈ V .
Thus the determinant records how T acts on the one-dimensional space of top-degree
alternating forms.
Example 80. Let
T =

1 2
3 4
act on R
2
, and let
ω
x1
y1

,

x2
y2
 = x1y2 − x2y1.
Since
ω(e1, e2) = 1,
we obtain
det T = ω(T e1, T e2) = ω
1
3

,

2
4
 = −2.
The definition immediately gives the behaviour of determinant under composition.
Proposition 130 (LADR 9.49(a)). Let S, T ∈ L(V ). Then
det(ST) = (det S)(det T).
Proof. For ω ∈ Altn(V ) and v1, . . . , vn ∈ V ,
ω(ST v1, . . . , ST vn) = (det S) ω(T v1, . . . , T vn)
= (det S)(det T) ω(v1, . . . , vn).
By the definition of determinant, the scalar on the right is det(ST).
Corollary 1. For T ∈ L(V ),
T is invertible ⇐⇒ det T ̸= 0.
If T is invertible, then
det(T
−1
) = (det T)
−1
.
186

Proof. First, det I = 1. If T is invertible, then
1 = det I = det(T T −1) = (det T)(det T
−1
),
so det T ̸= 0 and the displayed formula follows.
Conversely, suppose that T is not invertible. Because V is finite-dimensional, T is
not injective. Choose a nonzero v1 ∈ null(T) and extend it to a basis
v1, . . . , vn.
Choose a nonzero ω ∈ Altn(V ). Since a nonzero top-degree alternating form is nonzero
on every basis,
ω(v1, . . . , vn) ̸= 0.
But
(det T) ω(v1, . . . , vn) = ω(T v1, . . . , T vn) = 0.
Thus det T = 0.

The intrinsic determinant agrees with the matrix determinant already familiar from
Math 54.

For reference, the familiar explicit formula can be recovered very quickly from the

σ : {1, . . . , n} −→ {1, . . . , n}.
Its sign sgn(σ) is 1 or −1 according as the number of inversions of σ is even or odd.

If Sσ is the permutation matrix introduced in the Matrices chapter, then the Leibniz
formula has exactly one nonzero term and gives
det Sσ = sgn(σ).
Thus the sign of a permutation is also the determinant of the corresponding coordinaterearrangement operator.
The formula is useful for structural arguments, although row reduction is usually
the more efficient way to calculate a determinant.
Proposition 133. If A = (aij ) is upper triangular, then
det A = a11 · · · ann.
Proof. In a nonzero term of the Leibniz formula we must have
aσ(j),j ̸= 0 for every j.
Because A is upper triangular, this requires
σ(j) ≤ j for every j.
The only permutation with this property is the identity. Thus only the identity term
survives, and it is
a11 · · · ann.
Determinant, eigenvalues and the characteristic polynomial
The triangular formula gives the spectral interpretation of the determinant.
Proposition 134 (LADR 9.55). Let T ∈ L(V ), where V is a finite-dimensional
complex vector space. If
λ1, . . . , λn
are the eigenvalues of T, listed with algebraic multiplicity, then
det T = λ1 · · · λn.
Equivalently, if the distinct eigenvalues are µ1, . . . , µr with algebraic multiplicities
m1, . . . , mr, then
det T = µ
m1
1
· · · µ
mr
r
.
189

Proof. Choose a Jordan basis for T. The representing matrix is upper triangular,
and its diagonal entries are the eigenvalues of T, repeated according to their algebraic
multiplicities. The preceding proposition therefore gives the result.
This now proves, from the determinant theory developed in this chapter, the identity
that we used earlier when discussing the characteristic polynomial. On a complex vector
space we defined
χT (z) = Yn
j=1
(z − λj ),
with eigenvalues repeated according to algebraic multiplicity. Applying the preceding
proposition to zI − T gives
det(zI − T) = Yn
j=1
(z − λj ) = χT (z).
Thus the intrinsic characteristic polynomial introduced earlier is the familiar matrix
expression
χT (z) = det(zI − T) .
In particular, the constant term is
χT (0) = (−1)ndet T,
while the coefficient of z
n−1
is
−tr(T),
as we saw in the Trace chapter.
Real operators. Let T be an operator on a finite-dimensional real vector space. With
respect to a real basis, T and its complexification TC are represented by exactly the
same matrix. Hence
det
R
T = det
C
TC.
If
λ1, . . . , λn
are the eigenvalues of TC, counted with algebraic multiplicity, then
det
R
T = λ1 · · · λn.
The nonreal eigenvalues occur in conjugate pairs, so this product is real.
Recall that we defined the characteristic polynomial of a real operator to be the
characteristic polynomial of its complexification. The same argument therefore gives,
for a real representing matrix A,
χT (z) = det(zI − A).
The coefficients are real because the nonreal roots occur in conjugate pairs.
The determinant is also closely related to the singular value decomposition.

Singular values, volume and orientation
Let V be a finite-dimensional inner product space over R or C, and let
T ∈ L(V ).
Write
s1, . . . , sn
for the singular values of T, counted with multiplicity.
Proposition 135. The product of the singular values is the absolute value of the
determinant:
| det T| = s1 · · · sn .
Proof. The eigenvalues of T

For a real inner product space, this has a direct geometric interpretation. The
singular value decomposition writes a representing matrix as
A = UΣV

191

Consequently an n-dimensional parallelepiped has its volume multiplied by
s1 · · · sn = | det T|.
The sign of the determinant supplies the remaining information over R. A positive
determinant preserves orientation, a negative determinant reverses it, and
det T = 0
means that at least one singular value is zero, so T collapses the space into a lowerdimensional subspace.
Example 81. The map on R
2
represented by

−2 0
0 3
has singular values 2 and 3. Areas are therefore multiplied by
2 · 3 = 6 = | det T|.
Since
det T = −6,
the map also reverses orientation.
We have now encountered the determinant in several forms. For an n-dimensional
vector space, it is the scalar by which T acts on the one-dimensional space of top-degree
alternating forms. In a basis it is the usual determinant of the representing matrix. Over
C it is the product of the eigenvalues, counted with algebraic multiplicity, and on a real
inner product space its absolute value is the product of the singular values, hence the
factor by which T scales n-dimensional volume.
These descriptions fit together with the characteristic polynomial:
χT (z) = det(zI − T).
Its next-to-leading coefficient is
−tr(T),
and its constant term is
(−1)ndet T.
Thus trace and determinant give two basic scalar summaries of the operator. The
characteristic polynomial records the eigenvalues with their algebraic multiplicities.
